\displaystyle\times\ \prod_{w\in\Sigma^{\textrm{\tiny(2)}}}\,{\Gamma}\big(\mathcal{U}_{w},e^{2}\,a_{w}\big)\ ,

(2.69)

where
a=\sum_{w\in\Sigma^{\textrm{\tiny(2)}}}\,a_{w}
, the sum is
over (flat)
\mathsf{Out}(G)
-bundles on the triangulation of the
surface
\Sigma
,
N
is an appropriate normalisation power, and

{\Gamma}(\mathcal{U}_{w},e^{2}\,a_{w})
is the same local function
(
2.21
) as for the Yang-Mills theory based on the gauge
group
G
,
11
11

11

As in Section
2.3
, this is the case
because locally both gauge theories agree.
but now with the
holonomies
\mathcal{U}_{w}
computed for the
G\rtimes\mathsf{Out}(G)
-bundle with parallel transport
(g_{\gamma},\kappa_{\gamma})
along
the edges
\gamma\in\partial w
.
The quantity
\mathcal{W}_{D}\big((g_{\gamma},\kappa_{\gamma})\big)
is the value of the corresponding Wilson line observable for the
G\rtimes\mathsf{Out}(G)
-bundle described
by the elements
(g_{\gamma},\kappa_{\gamma})\in G\rtimes\mathsf{Out}(G)
for

\gamma\in\Sigma^{\textrm{\tiny(1)}}
, which can be computed as follows:
Combining the junction fields (
2.67
) for all vertices defines an element in

\mathbb{C}[\mathsf{Out}(G)]^{\otimes|\Sigma^{\textrm{\tiny(1)}}|}\otimes{L}^{2}(\mathsf{Out}(G))^{\otimes|\Sigma^{\textrm{\tiny(1)}}|}
.
To produce a complex number from this we act on the elements of

\mathbb{C}[\mathsf{Out}(G)]
with the group element of the corresponding edge, and
then apply to it the function in
L^{2}(\mathsf{Out}(G))
corresponding to the
endpoint of the edge; this defines
\mathcal{W}_{D}
.
The form of the junction fields (
2.67
) implies that an edge
\gamma_{x,y}

between two vertices
x,y\in\Sigma^{\textrm{\tiny(0)}}
induces a delta-function between the sums for the
different vertices of the form
\delta_{\kappa_{\gamma_{x,y}}\,\kappa_{x},\kappa_{y}}
. This implies that
\mathcal{W}_{D}
is
non-zero if and only if the parallel transport around every loop has
trivial part in
\mathsf{Out}(G)
.
In this case we can apply a gauge transformation to set all
\kappa_{\gamma}=\text{id}_{G}
.
Restricting to elements with all
\kappa_{\gamma}=\text{id}_{G}
cancels the factor
|\mathsf{Out}(G)|^{N}

in the partition function
W_{\textrm{\tiny YM}}
. Hence we are left with the partition function for
Yang-Mills theory with gauge group
G
, showing that the reverse orbifold
theory is the Yang-Mills theory we started with:

\displaystyle W_{\textrm{\tiny YM}}\big(\Sigma,G\rtimes\mathsf{Out}(G),e^{2}\,a;D\big)=Z_{\textrm{\tiny YM}}\big(\Sigma,G,e^{2}\,a\big)\ .

(2.70)

3
Generalised orbifold of functorial defect Yang-Mills theory

In this section we gauge the
\mathsf{Out}(G)
-symmetry of two-dimensional Yang-Mills theory
using the generalised orbifold construction

[
FFRS09
,
CR16
,
BCP14
,
CRS19
]

of a functorial defect quantum field theory.
We start by recalling the notion of area-dependent quantum field theories and their state sum constructions in
Section
3.1
. Then we give a detailed description of the
bicategory of topological defects of two-dimensional Yang-Mills theories in Section
3.2
, and in Section
3.3
we discuss the regularised Frobenius algebras constructed from a Lie group and its outer automorphism group.
Finally, in Section
3.4
we gauge the

\mathsf{Out}(G)
-symmetry, and using an orbifold equivalence in the orbifold
completion of the topological defect bicategory we give the defect for the reverse orbifold in Section
3.5
.

3.1
State sum area-dependent quantum field theory with defects

We begin by briefly reviewing the state sum construction of two-dimensional area-dependent quantum field theory with defects
[
RS21
]
. We define area-dependent quantum field theories in the spirit of
[
Ati88
,
Seg88
,
Seg89
]
as symmetric monoidal functors from a bordism category into an appropriate target category. Then we recall some details of the state sum construction,
and discuss transmissive defects which are the topological defects in area-dependent theories.

An
area-dependent quantum field theory
is a symmetric monoidal functor from the
category of two-dimensional bordisms with area
{\mathscr{B}\mathsf{ord}_{2}^{\hskip 1.0pt\textrm{area}}}
to the category of Hilbert spaces
{\mathscr{H}\mathsf{ilb}}
.
In the former category the objects are disjoint unions of oriented
circles, and the morphisms are oriented bordisms up
to diffeomorphism together with
a positive (and possibly zero for cylinders) real number assigned to each connected component, which we think of as an area. The morphism sets naturally come with a topology induced by the areas of the connected components of surfaces.
In the category of Hilbert spaces one can choose many different topologies on morphism sets,
but for our purposes the strong operator topology will be relevant.
For an area-dependent quantum field theory, in addition to being symmetric monoidal,
we require the bounded linear operators assigned to bordisms to be continuous in the area parameters.

Area-dependent quantum field theories (without defects) are completely
defined by a variation of the notion of a Frobenius algebra,
analogously to two-dimensional topological field theories. A
regularised Frobenius algebra
consists of a Hilbert space
A
together with families of maps

\mu_{a}:A\otimes A\longrightarrow A
(products),
\eta_{a}:\mathbb{C}\longrightarrow A
(units),

\Delta_{a}:A\longrightarrow A\otimes A
(coproducts) and
\varepsilon_{a}:A\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}
(counits) which are
continuous in the parameter
a\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
with respect to the strong operator topology.
These are required to satisfy parameterised versions of
associativity, unitality, coassociativity, and counitality:

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \mu_{a}\circ(\mu_{b}\otimes\text{id}_{A})&=\mu_{a^{\prime}}\circ(\text{id}_{A}\otimes\mu_{b^{\prime}})\ ,\quad&\mu_{a}\circ\left(\eta_{b}\otimes\text{id}_{A}\right)&=\mu_{a^{\prime}}\circ\left(\text{id}_{A}\otimes\eta_{b^{\prime}}\right)=:P_{a+b}\ ,\\[4.0pt]
\left(\Delta_{b}\otimes\text{id}_{A}\right)\circ\Delta_{a}&=\left(\text{id}_{A}\otimes\Delta_{b^{\prime}}\right)\circ\Delta_{a^{\prime}}\ ,\quad&\left(\varepsilon_{b}\otimes\text{id}_{A}\right)\circ\Delta_{a}&=\left(\text{id}_{A}\otimes\varepsilon_{b^{\prime}}\right)\circ\Delta_{a^{\prime}}=P_{a+b}\ ,\end{aligned}

(3.1)

and of the Frobenius relation

\displaystyle\Delta_{a}\circ\mu_{b}=(\text{id}_{A}\otimes\mu_{b^{\prime}})\circ(\Delta_{a^{\prime}}\otimes\text{id}_{A})=(\mu_{b^{\prime}}\otimes\text{id}_{A})\circ(\text{id}_{A}\otimes\Delta_{a^{\prime}})\ ,

(3.2)

for all parameters
a,a^{\prime},b,b^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
with
a+b=a^{\prime}+b^{\prime}
,
where the map
P_{a}:A\longrightarrow A
satisfies

\displaystyle\lim_{a\to 0}\,P_{a}=\text{id}_{A}

(3.3)

in the strong operator topology.

We will heavily rely on the graphical calculus for (strict) symmetric
monoidal categories, in order to simplify the presentation of our calculations.
We present a morphism
f:A\longrightarrow B
as

f
B
A

(3.4)

and the identity morphisms with a straight line

A
\text{id}_{A}=
A

(3.5)

Composition corresponds to stacking,
the tensor product of objects and morphisms is

f

B

(3.7)

For more details on this graphical calculus, see for example
[
Kas94
]
.

Area-dependent quantum field theories are classified by commutative regularised Frobenius algebras:
the underlying Hilbert space is the value of the quantum field theory on the circle
S^{1}
, and
the structure maps are the values on the generating morphisms of
{\mathscr{B}\mathsf{ord}_{2}^{\hskip 1.0pt\textrm{area}}}
, which are the cups, caps and pairs of pants. For further details see
[
RS21
, Section 3.2]
.

An
area-dependent quantum field theory with defects
is a symmetric monoidal functor from the category
of two-dimensional bordisms with area and defects. In this bordism category we endow manifolds with a stratification, which is a collection of immersed manifolds of lower dimension. The surface components are assigned individual areas and the functor is required to be continuous in all of these area parameters.

The category of bordisms with area and defects comes with three label sets
D_{2}
,
D_{1}
and
D_{0}
, which respectively label the submanifolds of dimension two, one and zero. The elements of
D_{2}
are
called phases, the elements of
D_{1}
are called domain walls or defect conditions, and
the elements of
D_{0}
are called junction field labels. For more details see for example
[
DKR11
]
and
[
RS21
, Section 3.3]
.

Consider an area-dependent quantum field theory with defects
\mathcal{Z}
.
A defect line labeled with
x\in D_{1}
is
transmissive
if the value of
\mathcal{Z}

on surfaces involving defects labeled with
x
depends only on the sum of the areas of the surface components separated by the defect; in other words, area can be transmitted through the defect line. These are the topological defects in area-dependent quantum field theories.
When only considering topological defects, the sets
D_{0}
,
D_{1}
and
D_{2}
can be organised
into a bicategory using the functor
\mathcal{Z}
, see Section
3.2
below for further
details.

One way to construct examples of area-dependent quantum field theory with defects is using the
‘state sum construction’. Here one works with an appropriate cell decomposition of the surface; for example, faces are allowed to be intersected by defect lines (without junctions)
at most once, and junctions and faces should intersect at most once.

The set labeling surface components
D_{2}
is a set of strongly separable symmetric
Frobenius algebras.
A regularised Frobenius algebra
A
is
symmetric
if the natural bilinear pairings

\varepsilon_{a}\circ\mu_{b}:A\otimes A\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}
are symmetric:

\varepsilon_{a}\circ\mu_{b}\circ\sigma_{A,A}=\varepsilon_{a}\circ\mu_{b}
,
and it is
strongly separable
if there exist algebra homomorphisms
\tau_{a}:A\longrightarrow A
which satisfy

\tau_{a}\circ\mu_{b}\circ\Delta_{c}=\mu_{b}\circ\Delta_{c}\circ\tau_{a}=P_{a+b+c}

for every
a,b,c\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
. Using such Frobenius algebras ensures
that the state sum construction will be independent of the choice of
cell decomposition.
The examples of Frobenius algebras considered in this paper are strongly separable symmetric with
\tau_{a}=P_{a}
.

Before we can describe the set
D_{1}
, we need to define bimodules.
A

Consider a boundary circle of a surface with defect lines, some of which start or end on

,
the state space assigned to this circle is

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}(S^{1},A_{1},\dots,A_{n};X_{1},\dots,X_{n})=\ \circlearrowleft_{A_{1}}X_{1}^{\epsilon_{1}}\otimes_{A_{2}}X_{2}^{\epsilon_{2}}\otimes_{A_{3}}\dots\otimes_{A_{n}}X_{n}^{\epsilon_{n}}\ ,

(3.21)

where
A_{i}\in D_{2}
,
X_{i}\in D_{1}
, and
\epsilon_{i}\in\left\{\pm\right\}

depending on the orientation of the
i
-th defect, with

X_{i}^{+}=X_{i}
and
X_{i}^{-}=\bar{X}_{i}
the dual of
X_{i}
.

The set
D_{0}
of junction field labels is given by families of elements in the state spaces (
3.21
) which are invariant under the action of cylinders
over the circles, which are cylinders with parallel defect lines. We give more detail on this in Section
3.2
below.

Now we sketch what the state sum construction assigns to a surface
\Sigma:S\longrightarrow T
with defect lines.
We pick a cell decomposition of the surface such that the defect lines intersect only edges and they intersect each edge at most once. We require that every face contains at most one junction of defect lines.
Then we define
\mathcal{Z}(\Sigma)
in two steps.
First we consider the surface
\Sigma^{\prime}
obtained from
\Sigma
by cutting out small disks near
the junctions, and we regard the new boundary components as ingoing.
Then we compose
\mathcal{Z}(\Sigma^{\prime})
with
\text{id}_{\mathcal{Z}(S)}
tensored with the elements from
D_{0}
that label the junction fields.

It remains to show how to define
\mathcal{Z}(\Sigma^{\prime})
, which is
the value of the functor
\mathcal{Z}
on surfaces without junctions of defects. To each face we assign the morphism

X

\longmapsto
\bar{X}

(3.22)

\varepsilon_{a_{1}}\circ\mu_{a_{2}}
, to contract legs corresponding to inner edges
according to the cell decomposition (which describes how faces are
glued together along the edges indicated by dashed lines)
and to define ingoing edges.
Finally we compose with the corresponding projections and embeddings to the state space.

3.2
The defect bicategory of Yang-Mills theory

For the remainder of this paper we focus on the area-dependent quantum
field theory
\mathcal{Z}=\mathcal{Z}_{\textrm{\tiny YM}}
corresponding to
two-dimensional Yang-Mills theory, as defined in
Section
1
; in this case the state sum construction
provides a rigorous implementation of the lattice regularisation of
Section
2
. Compared to Section
2
, in
the following we set the gauge coupling constant to
e=1
without loss
of generality. The weak-coupling limit, which determines a topological
field theory, is then equivalent to the zero
area limit
a\longrightarrow 0
.

We define a bicategory of topological defects
\mathscr{B}_{\textsf{\tiny YM}}
in the spirit of
[
DKR11
,
CR16
]
.
This bicategory has as objects regularised Frobenius algebras of the form
A=L^{2}(G)
where
G
is a
compact semi-simple Lie group. The 1-morphisms
X:A\longrightarrow B
are transmissive bimodules with duals
and the composition is given by the relative tensor product.
The 2-morphisms
X\Longrightarrow Y
for
X,Y:A\longrightarrow B
are given by the set of families
of maps
\{\phi_{a}:\mathbb{C}\longrightarrow\ \circlearrowleft_{A}Y\otimes_{B}\bar{X}\}_{a\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}}

which are invariant under the action of cylinders:
12
12

12

This
definition is an incarnation of the operator-state correspondence of
local quantum field theory.

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{b}\circ\phi_{a}=\phi_{a+b}\ ,

For any morphism

in this case

However, there exist invariant families for which this limit does not

\displaystyle\big\{\eta^{C\ell^{2}(G)}_{a}:\mathbb{C}\longrightarrow\ \circlearrowleft_{L^{2}(G)}L^{2}(G)\otimes_{L^{2}(G)}L^{2}(G)\simeq C\ell^{2}(G)\big\}_{a\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}}\ .

Let
\mathsf{Hom}_{A|B}^{\mathrm{fam}}(X,Y)
denote the families of bimodule morphisms

\{\psi_{a}:X\longrightarrow Y\}_{a\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}}
which satisfy the invariance property

\displaystyle Q_{b}^{Y}\circ\psi_{a}=\psi_{a}\circ Q_{b}^{X}=\psi_{a+b}\ .

(3.34)

The composition of two families of bimodule morphisms

\{\xi_{a}:Y\longrightarrow Z\}_{a\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}}\in\mathsf{Hom}_{A|B}^{\mathrm{fam}}(Y,Z)
and

\{\psi_{b}:X\longrightarrow Y\}_{b\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}}\in\mathsf{Hom}_{A|B}^{\mathrm{fam}}(X,Y)
is defined via the pointwise composition

\displaystyle\left\{\xi_{a}\right\}_{a\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}}\circ\left\{\psi_{b}\right\}_{b\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}}:=\left\{\xi_{a}\circ\psi_{b}\right\}_{a+b\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}}\ \in\ \mathsf{Hom}_{A|B}^{\mathrm{fam}}(X,Z)\ .

(3.35)

This composition is well defined, as it is independent of the choice of parameters
a
and
b
.
The unit for this composition is the family defined via the
identity morphisms of the respective bimodules.
Similarly we define the relative tensor product
of two families
\left\{\psi_{a}:X\longrightarrow Y\right\}_{a\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}}\in\mathsf{Hom}_{A|B}^{\mathrm{fam}}(X,Y)
and

pointwise by

\displaystyle\left\{\psi_{a}\right\}_{a\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}}\otimes_{B}\left\{\psi^{\prime}_{b}\right\}_{b\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}}:=\left\{\psi_{a}\otimes_{B}\psi^{\prime}_{b}\right\}_{a+b\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}}\ \in\ \mathsf{Hom}_{A|C}^{\mathrm{fam}}(X\otimes_{B}X^{\prime},Y\otimes_{B}Y^{\prime})\ ,

(3.36)

which is again well defined.
The units for this tensor product are the families

in

(3.42)

For the horizontal composition we have

\psi
\pi
\psi^{\prime}
\pi
\mathcal{S}(\psi)\circ^{\mathrm{hor}}\mathcal{S}(\psi^{\prime})=
\iota
\iota
=
\pi
\circlearrowleft_{B}\bar{X}\otimes_{A}Y\otimes_{B}Y^{\prime}\otimes_{C}\bar{X^{\prime}}
\psi
\psi^{\prime}
=
\pi
\circlearrowleft_{B}\bar{X}\otimes_{A}Y\otimes_{B}Y^{\prime}\otimes_{C}\bar{X^{\prime}}
\psi
\psi^{\prime}
\pi
\circlearrowleft_{B}\bar{X}\otimes_{A}Y\otimes_{B}Y^{\prime}\otimes_{C}\bar{X^{\prime}}
\pi
\pi

=\mathcal{S}(\psi\otimes_{B}\psi^{\prime})\ ,
=
\psi
\psi^{\prime}
=
\pi
\circlearrowleft_{B}\bar{X}\otimes_{A}Y\otimes_{B}Y^{\prime}\otimes_{C}\bar{X^{\prime}}
\pi
\pi
\psi
\psi^{\prime}
\pi
\circlearrowleft_{B}\bar{X}\otimes_{A}Y\otimes_{B}Y^{\prime}\otimes_{C}\bar{X^{\prime}}
\pi
\iota

(3.43)

where we used the cyclicity of the tensor product

\circlearrowleft_{B}\bar{X}\otimes_{A}Y\otimes_{B}Y^{\prime}\otimes_{C}\bar{X^{\prime}}

together with the fact that the relative tensor product of morphisms can be expressed
using the projections
\pi
and embeddings
\iota
.
∎

Lemma
3.37
gives two equivalent ways of thinking about the 2-morphisms in the
bicategory of topological defects.
Sometimes it is easier to work with families of bimodule morphisms as their
weak-coupling limit exists more frequently, for example the families
\{\eta_{a}^{A}\}_{a\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}}
,

\big\{Q_{a}^{X}\big\}_{a\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}}
and

\big\{P_{a}^{A}\big\}_{a\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}}
, and we can compute with the
limits instead of the families.
On the other hand, in general the weak-coupling limit of an invariant family
may not exist: Take for example the Frobenius algebra
A=\bigoplus_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\,\mathbb{C}\,e_{k}
with orthonormal basis
\left\{e_{k}\right\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
,
product
\mu(e_{j}\otimes e_{k})=\delta_{jk}\,e_{k}
and unit

\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\,\e^{-a\,k^{2}}\,e_{k}
, and consider
A
as a
bimodule over itself. Then the family of endomorphisms

\left\{\phi_{a}\right\}_{a\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}}
of
A
given by
\phi_{a}(e_{k})=\e^{-a\,k^{2}}\,k^{2}\,e_{k}

clearly does not have a weak-coupling limit.

Accordingly we can now give a working definition.

Definition 3.44
.

The
topological defect bicategory
\mathscr{B}_{\textsf{\tiny YM}}
of
two-dimensional Yang-Mills theories
has:

(a)

Objects:
Hilbert spaces
L^{2}(G)
for
G
compact
semi-simple Lie groups with regularised Frobenius algebra structure given by (
1.1
);

(b)

1-morphisms:
Transmissive bimodules with duals
between regularised Frobenius algebras
L^{2}(G)
and

L^{2}(H)
; and

(c)

2-morphisms:
Invariant families of bimodule morphisms.

In order to apply techniques from
[
CR16
]
later on
we will need

Proposition 3.45
.

The bicategory
\mathscr{B}_{\textsf{\tiny YM}}
is idempotent complete; that is, its morphism categories are idempotent complete.

Proof.

Let
\psi=\left\{\psi_{a}:X\longrightarrow X\right\}_{a\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}}
be an idempotent on
an
A
–
B
-bimodule
X
, that is it obeys

\psi_{a}\circ\psi_{b}=\psi_{a+b}
.
Let
Y
be the closure of the subspace
\bigcup_{a\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}}\,\im(\psi_{a})
,

p:X\longrightarrow Y
the projection and

e:Y\longrightarrow X
the embedding of the subspace
Y\subset X
.
The Hilbert space
Y
becomes an
A
–
B
-bimodule via the induced action

p\circ\rho^{X}\circ(\text{id}_{A}\otimes e\otimes\text{id}_{B})
.
Since
\psi_{a}
is an intertwiner, the action on
X
indeed restricts to
Y
.
Set
\pi:=\{p_{a}=p\circ\psi_{a}:X\longrightarrow Y\}_{a\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}}
and

\iota:=\{e_{a}=\psi_{a}\circ e:Y\longrightarrow X\}_{a\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}}
.
Then
\iota\circ\pi=\psi
and
\pi\circ\iota=\text{id}_{Y}
; the first
equation is clear from the definition of
p
and
e
, while
the second equation follows from
\psi_{a}(y)=Q_{a}^{Y}(y)
for
y\in Y
by the definition of
Y
.
∎

Example 3.46
.

Wilson lines can be described by bimodules over
L^{2}(G)
as follows.
Let
V
be a representation of
G
and consider

V\otimes L^{2}(G)
with the commuting left and right actions

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \psi\cdot(v\otimes f)=\left[x\longmapsto\int_{G}\,\mathrm{d}y\ \psi(y)\,(y\cdot v)\,f(y^{-1}\,x)\right]\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad(v\otimes f)\cdot\psi=v\otimes(f*\psi)\ ,\end{aligned}

(3.47)

for
v\in V
and
\psi,f\in L^{2}(G)
.
Similarly,
L^{2}(G)\otimes V
is a bimodule via

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \psi\cdot(f\otimes v)=(\psi*f)\otimes v\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad(f\otimes v)\cdot\psi=\left[x\longmapsto\int_{G}\,\mathrm{d}y\ (y^{-1}\cdot v)\,f(x\,y^{-1})\,\psi(y)\right]\ .\end{aligned}

(3.48)

The dual of
V\otimes L^{2}(G)
is
L^{2}(G)\otimes V^{*}
, where
V^{*}
is the
dual of
V
.
If
G
is connected then Wilson lines are
not
transmissive,
so they are not 1-morphisms in
\mathscr{B}_{\textsf{\tiny YM}}
.
Nevertheless we will need these for disconnected gauge groups.
For further details on Wilson lines see
[
RS21
, Proposition 5.10]
.

Example 3.49
.

The twisted bimodules
L_{\varphi}=L^{2}(G)
for
\varphi\in\mathsf{Out}(G)
with
action given in (
1.4
)
are transmissive bimodules over
L^{2}(G)
, so they are 1-morphisms in
\mathscr{B}_{\textsf{\tiny YM}}
.

3.3
Frobenius algebras from symmetry defects

Next we define regularised Frobenius algebras and their bimodules in
the category

{\mathscr{H}\mathsf{ilb}}
, starting from a Lie group and its outer automorphism group.
Let
G
be a compact semi-simple Lie group and

\Gamma<\mathsf{Out}(G)
a subgroup of outer automorphisms of
G
.
We will sometimes use the notation
L=L^{2}(G)
,
H=L^{2}(\Gamma)

and
K=L^{2}(G\rtimes\Gamma)
for brevity.

The group
\mathsf{Out}(G)
is finite and the algebra

L^{2}(\Gamma)
is isomorphic to
the group algebra of
\Gamma
, which has the structure of a Hopf algebra
with coproduct

(3.54)

for
f,g\in L^{2}(G)
and

.
We define the coproduct and counit to be the adjoint operators of the product and unit respectively.
We denote this Frobenius algebra by

.

(3.55)

is a homomorphism of regularised Frobenius algebras.
Using this morphism we obtain an
L^{2}(G)
–
L^{2}(G)
-bimodule structure on

L^{2}(G)\rtimes L^{2}(\Gamma)
.

Proposition 3.56
.

1.

The map

\displaystyle\Phi:L\rtimes H\longrightarrow K\ ,\quad f\otimes\phi\longmapsto\big[(x,\gamma)\mapsto f(x)\,\phi_{\gamma}\big]

(3.57)

is an isomorphism of regularised Frobenius algebras in
{\mathscr{H}\mathsf{ilb}}
. The map (
3.55
) endows

K
with the structure of a transmissive
L
–
L
-bimodule.

2.

The
L
–
L
-bimodule
K

is a strongly separable symmetric Frobenius algebra
in
\mathscr{B}_{\textsf{\tiny YM}}(L,L)
via the structure morphisms

\displaystyle\bar{\mu}{}^{K}

\displaystyle:=\big\{K\otimes_{L}K\xrightarrow{\ \iota\ }K\otimes K\xrightarrow{\ \mu_{a}\ }K\big\}_{a\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}}\ ,

\displaystyle\bar{\Delta}{}^{K}

\displaystyle:=\big\{K\xrightarrow{\ \Delta_{a}\ }K\otimes K\xrightarrow{\ \pi\ }K\otimes_{L}K\big\}_{a\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}}\ ,

\displaystyle\bar{\eta}{}^{K}

\displaystyle:=\big\{L\xrightarrow{\ \bar{\eta}_{a}\ }K\big\}_{a\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}}\ ,\quad\bar{\eta}_{a}(f)=P_{a}^{L}(f)\otimes|\Gamma|\,\delta_{\text{id}_{G}}\ ,

\displaystyle\bar{\varepsilon}{}^{K}

\displaystyle:=\big\{K\xrightarrow{\ \bar{\varepsilon}_{a}\ }L\big\}_{a\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}}\ ,\quad\bar{\varepsilon}_{a}(f\otimes\phi)=P_{a}^{L}(f)\,\frac{1}{|\Gamma|}\,\phi_{\text{id}_{G}}\ .

(3.58)

3.

The
L
–
L
-bimodule
M:=\bigoplus_{\varphi\in\Gamma}\,L_{\varphi}
is a strongly separable symmetric Frobenius algebra
in
\mathscr{B}_{\textsf{\tiny YM}}(L,L)
via the structure morphisms

\displaystyle\bar{\mu}{}^{M}

\displaystyle:=\Big\{M\otimes_{L}M\xrightarrow{\ \iota\ }M\otimes M\xrightarrow{\sum\limits_{\varphi,\omega\in\Gamma}\,\mu_{a}^{\varphi,\omega}}M\Big\}_{a\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}}\ ,

\displaystyle\bar{\Delta}{}^{M}

\displaystyle:=\Big\{M\xrightarrow{\frac{1}{|\Gamma|}\,\sum\limits_{\varphi,\omega\in\Gamma}\,\Delta_{a}^{\varphi,\omega}}M\otimes M\xrightarrow{\ \pi\ }M\otimes_{L}M\Big\}_{a\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}}\ ,

\displaystyle\bar{\eta}{}^{M}

\displaystyle:=\Big\{L\xrightarrow{\ P^{L}_{a}\ }L=L_{\text{id}_{G}}\xhookrightarrow{\ ~~\ }\bigoplus_{\varphi\in\Gamma}\,L_{\varphi}=M\Big\}_{a\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}}\ ,

\displaystyle\bar{\varepsilon}{}^{M}

\displaystyle:=\Big\{M=\bigoplus_{\varphi\in\Gamma}\,L_{\varphi}\twoheadrightarrow L_{\text{id}_{G}}=L\xrightarrow{\ P^{L}_{a}\ }L\Big\}_{a\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}}\ ,

(3.59)

where

\displaystyle{\hbox to69.17pt{\vbox to66.53pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 56.11148pt\lower-31.48679pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \hbox to0.0pt{
{

{
{}{}{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}
{{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}}{
{}{}{}}{{{}}{{}}}{
{}{}{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 4.92 4.92 C 4.92 10.37 0.53 14.76 -4.92 14.76}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{
{}{}{}}{}{{}}{}
{
{}{}{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}

(3.60)

4.

The map

\displaystyle\Psi:\bigoplus_{\varphi\in\Gamma}\,L_{\varphi}\longrightarrow K\ ,\quad\sum_{\varphi\in\Gamma}\,f_{\varphi}\longmapsto\big[(x,\gamma)\mapsto f_{\gamma}\big(\gamma^{-1}(x)\big)\big]

(3.61)

is an isomorphism of
L
–
L
-bimodules as well as of
Frobenius algebras in
\mathscr{B}_{\textsf{\tiny YM}}(L,L)
.

Proof.

For Part
1
, we note that

\Delta_{H}(\delta_{\varphi})=|\Gamma|\,\delta_{\varphi}\otimes\delta_{\varphi}

and
|\Gamma|\,\delta_{\varphi}\cdot f=f\circ\varphi^{-1}
.
Using
(
3.54
)
we first compute

\displaystyle\Phi\big((f\otimes\delta_{\varphi})*(g\otimes\phi)\big)(x,\omega)

\displaystyle=\Phi\big((f*|\Gamma|\,\delta_{\varphi}\cdot g)\otimes(\delta_{\varphi}*\phi)\big)(x,\omega)

\displaystyle=\big(f*(g\circ\varphi^{-1})\big)(x)\,\frac{1}{|\Gamma|}\,\phi_{\varphi^{-1}\,\omega}\ .

(3.62)

Then we compute

\displaystyle\big(\Phi(f\otimes\delta_{\varphi})*\Phi(g\otimes\phi)\big)(x,\omega)

\displaystyle=\int_{G}\,\mathrm{d}y\ \frac{1}{|\Gamma|}\,\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma}\,f(y)\,\delta_{\varphi}(\gamma)\,g\big(\gamma^{-1}(y^{-1}\,x)\big)\,\phi_{\gamma^{-1}\,\omega}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{|\Gamma|}\,\big(f*(g\circ\varphi^{-1})\big)(x)\,\phi_{\varphi^{-1}\,\omega}\ ,

(3.63)

which agrees with (
3.62
).

Next we show that
\Phi\big(\eta_{a}^{L^{2}(G)}\otimes|\Gamma|\,\delta_{\text{id}_{G}}\big)
is
a unit for this multiplication, which by uniqueness of the unit of a regularised algebra is precisely
\eta_{a}^{L^{2}(G\rtimes\Gamma)}
.
For
F=f\otimes\phi\in K
we compute

\displaystyle\Big(\Phi\big(\eta_{a}^{L^{2}(G)}\otimes|\Gamma|\,\delta_{\text{id}_{G}}\big)*(f\otimes\phi)\Big)(x,\omega)

\displaystyle=\int_{G}\,\mathrm{d}y\ \frac{1}{|\Gamma|}\,\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma}\,\eta_{a}^{L^{2}(G)}(y)\,|\Gamma|\,\delta_{\text{id}_{G}}(\gamma)\,f\big(\gamma^{-1}(y^{-1}\,x)\big)\,\phi_{\gamma^{-1}\,\omega}

\displaystyle=\big(\eta_{a}^{L^{2}(G)}*f\big)(x)\,\phi_{\omega}\ ,

(3.64)

and

\displaystyle\Big((f\otimes\phi)*\Phi\big(\eta_{a}^{L^{2}(G)}\otimes|\Gamma|\,\delta_{\text{id}_{G}}\big)\Big)(x,\omega)

\displaystyle=\int_{G}\,\mathrm{d}y\ \frac{1}{|\Gamma|}\,\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma}\,f(y)\,\phi_{\gamma}\,\eta_{a}^{L^{2}(G)}\big(\gamma^{-1}(y^{-1}\,x)\big)\,|\Gamma|\,\delta_{\text{id}_{G}}\big(\gamma^{-1}\,\omega\big)

\displaystyle=\big(f\ast\eta_{a}^{L^{2}(G)}\big)(x)\,\phi_{\omega}\ ,

(3.65)

both of which are equal to
F(x,\omega)
in the
a\longrightarrow 0
limit, showing that

\Phi\big(\eta_{a}^{L^{2}(G)}\otimes|\Gamma|\,\delta_{\text{id}_{G}}\big)
is the unit of
K
.

Part
2
follows from the fact
that
K
is a strongly separable symmetric Frobenius algebra and from Part
1
.
We only present the computation which shows associativity.
Let us compute the
a+b+c
component of the family
\bar{\mu}{}^{K}\circ(\bar{\mu}{}^{K}\otimes_{L}\text{id}_{K})
:

\iota
\iota
K\otimes_{L}K\otimes_{L}K
P_{b}
\pi
\iota
K
\mu_{c}
\mu_{a}
\iota
K\otimes_{L}K\otimes_{L}K
P_{b}
K
\mu_{c}
\mu_{a}
=

(3.66)

where we used associativity of the relative tensor product and

\mu_{c}\circ\mathcal{D}_{0}=\mu_{c}
.
Computing the
a+b+c
component of the family

\bar{\mu}{}^{K}\circ(\text{id}_{K}\otimes_{L}\bar{\mu}{}^{K})
in a similar way gives the same family.

For Parts
3
and
4
,
the verification that the isomorphism is an
L
–
L
-bimodule morphism
follows from a direct computation:
For the left
L
-action we have

\displaystyle\Psi\Big(g\cdot\sum_{\varphi\in\Gamma}\,f_{\varphi}\Big)

\displaystyle=\Psi\Big(\sum_{\varphi\in\Gamma}\,f_{\varphi}*(g\circ\varphi)\Big)

\displaystyle=\Psi\Big(\sum_{\varphi\in\Gamma}\,\big(f_{\varphi}\circ\varphi^{-1}\big)*g\Big)

\displaystyle=\big[(x,\gamma)\longmapsto\big((f_{\gamma}\circ\gamma^{-1})*g\big)(x)\big]

\displaystyle=g\cdot\Psi\Big(\sum_{\varphi\in\Gamma}\,f_{\varphi}\Big)\ .

(3.67)

That
\Psi
commutes with the right
L
-action can be similarly shown using

\displaystyle(f\otimes\phi)\cdot g=\big[(x,\gamma)\longmapsto\big(f*(g\circ\gamma^{-1})\big)(x)\,\phi_{\gamma}\big]\ .

(3.68)

Finally, we compare the Frobenius algebra structure on
M
defined in
(
3.59
) with the structure transported from

K
via
\Psi
. Here we show that the product and coproduct agree.
Since the
a\longrightarrow 0
limits of these families exist,
it is enough to check
\Psi\circ P_{a}^{M}=P_{a}^{K}\circ\Psi

and compare the limits; this indeed holds, as it is given by
scaling basis elements by factors
\e^{-a\,C_{2}(\alpha)/2}
. For the product we compute

\displaystyle\Psi^{-1}\bigg(\Psi\Big(\sum_{\varphi\in\Gamma}\,f_{\varphi}\Big)

\displaystyle*\Psi\Big(\sum_{\omega\in\Gamma}\,g_{\omega}\big)\bigg)

\displaystyle=\Psi^{-1}\bigg(\Big[(x,\gamma)\longmapsto\int_{G}\,\mathrm{d}y\ \frac{1}{|\Gamma|}\,\sum_{\kappa\in\Gamma}\,f_{\kappa}\big(\kappa^{-1}(y)\big)\,g_{\kappa^{-1}\,\gamma}\big(\gamma^{-1}\,\kappa\,\kappa^{-1}(y^{-1}\,x)\big)\Big]\bigg)

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt=\Big[(x,\gamma)\longmapsto\int_{G}\,\mathrm{d}z\ \frac{1}{|\Gamma|}\,\sum_{\sigma\in\Gamma}\,f_{\gamma\,\sigma^{-1}}\big(\sigma(z)\big)\,g_{\sigma}\big(z^{-1}\,x\big)\Big]\ ,

(3.69)

where in the last step we changed summation and integration variables
(y,\kappa)=(1,\gamma)\,{\textrm{\tiny$\bullet$}}\,(z,\sigma^{-1})
.
This is exactly the product defined in (
3.59
).
For the coproduct we compute

\displaystyle\big(\Psi^{-1}\otimes\Psi^{-1}\big)\bigg[\Delta\bigg(\Psi\Big(\sum_{\varphi\in\Gamma}\,f_{\varphi}\Big)\bigg)\bigg]

\displaystyle=\big(\Psi^{-1}\otimes\Psi^{-1}\big)\Big(\big[\big((x,\gamma)\,,\,(y,\kappa)\big)\longmapsto f_{\gamma\,\kappa}\big((\gamma\,\kappa)^{-1}\,(x\,\gamma(y))\big)\big]\Big)

\displaystyle=\big[\big((x,\gamma)\,,\,(y,\kappa)\big)\longmapsto f_{\gamma\,\kappa}\big(\kappa^{-1}(x)\,y\big)\big]\ ,

(3.70)

which is exactly the coproduct described in (
3.59
).
∎

3.4
Gauging the
\mathsf{Out}(G)
-symmetry

We shall now compute the orbifold of two-dimensional Yang-Mills theory with gauge group
G
using a defect corresponding to the
\mathsf{Out}(G)
-symmetry. We do this by introducing labels
for defect junctions, and then compute the image of the projector which is the state space of the orbifold theory.
In order to verify that the orbifold theory is again a two-dimensional Yang-Mills theory
— with a different gauge group — we compute the orbifold theory on the generators of
the category of bordisms with area and without defects
{\mathscr{B}\mathsf{ord}_{2}^{\hskip 1.0pt\textrm{area}}}
, which is given
by the commutative Frobenius algebra
structure on the state space.

The class
[\Phi]\in H^{3}(\mathsf{Out}(G),\mathbb{C}^{\times})
obtained from the associator of a morphism category of the topological defect bicategory
\mathscr{B}_{\textsf{\tiny YM}}
is trivial,
[\Phi]=1
, so we can gauge any subgroup

\Gamma<\mathsf{Out}(G)

[
FFRS09
, Section 3]
, which we now fix.
In order to compute the orbifold theory of two-dimensional Yang-Mills theory with defect

M=\bigoplus_{\varphi\in\Gamma}\,L_{\varphi}
, we need to
give labels for junctions of the defect labeled with
M
.
It is enough to give the labels for trivalent junctions because, owing
to the fact that
M
is a strongly separable symmetric Frobenius algebra
in
\mathscr{B}_{\textsf{\tiny YM}}(L,L)
, the value of the area-dependent quantum field theory
on a surface with a defect network is invariant under certain changes of
the defect network, which allow us to define junction fields with higher valency.

To a trivalent junction with an ingoing defect labeled with

L_{\varphi}
and two outgoing defects labeled with
L_{\omega}
and
L_{\gamma}
,
as in Figure
6

a)
,
we assign the family

\displaystyle\Big\{\frac{1}{|\Gamma|}\,\delta_{\varphi,\gamma\,\omega}\,\eta_{a}^{L^{2}(G)}\Big\}_{a\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}}\ \in\ \ \circlearrowleft_{L}\bar{L}_{\varphi}\otimes_{L}{L}_{\omega}\otimes_{L}L_{\varphi\,\omega}\simeq C\ell^{2}(G)\ ,

(3.71)

where
a=a_{1}+a_{2}+a_{3}
is the total area of the three individual surface
components.

Next we turn to the projector of

whose image is

Proposition 3.79
.

1.

The projector
\mathcal{P}
is given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}=\frac{1}{|\Gamma|}\,\sum_{\omega\in\Gamma}\,\big(\omega^{-1}\big)^{*}:\mathcal{H}\longrightarrow\mathcal{H}\ ,

(3.80)

which implements an action of
\Gamma
on
\mathcal{H}
.

2.

The image of
\mathcal{P}
is the subspace
\mathcal{H}^{\Gamma}
of

\Gamma
-invariants under this action and there is an isomorphism

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{\Gamma}\simeq C\ell^{2}(G\rtimes\Gamma)\ .

(3.81)

Proof.

For Part

, take a cell

Summing over

we get (

).

For Part
2
, let
h\in\mathcal{H}^{\Gamma}
and write
h=\sum_{\varphi\in\Gamma}\,h_{\varphi}

for its components.
We define the map

\displaystyle\Psi:\mathcal{H}^{\Gamma}\longrightarrow C\ell^{2}(G\rtimes\Gamma)\ ,\quad h\longmapsto\big[(x,\alpha)\mapsto h_{\alpha}(x)\big]\ .

(3.83)

We show that the image of the map
\Psi
indeed lands in
C\ell^{2}(G\rtimes\Gamma)
.
Since
h
is invariant under the action of
\Gamma
we have

\displaystyle\omega\cdot h=\sum_{\varphi\in\Gamma}\,\omega\cdot h_{\varphi}=\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma}\,h_{\gamma}=h\ .

(3.84)

Because
\omega\cdot h_{\varphi}\in\mathcal{H}_{\omega\,\varphi\,\omega^{-1}}
,
we get

\displaystyle h_{\varphi}\circ\omega^{-1}=\omega\cdot h_{\varphi}=h_{\omega\,\varphi\,\omega^{-1}}\ .

(3.85)

Let
(x,\varphi),(y,\omega)\in G\rtimes\Gamma
and
f:=\Psi(h)
.
Then

\displaystyle f\big((x,\varphi)\,{\textrm{\tiny$\bullet$}}\,(y,\omega)\,{\textrm{\tiny$\bullet$}}\,(x,\varphi)^{-1}\big)

\displaystyle=f\big(x\,\varphi(y)\,(\varphi\,\omega\,\varphi^{-1})(x^{-1})\,,\,\varphi\,\omega\,\varphi^{-1}\big)

\displaystyle:=h_{\varphi\,\omega\,\varphi^{-1}}\big(x\,\varphi(y)\,(\varphi\,\omega\,\varphi^{-1})(x^{-1})\big)

\displaystyle=h_{\varphi\,\omega\,\varphi^{-1}}\big(\varphi(y)\big)

\displaystyle=h_{\omega}(y)

\displaystyle=:f(y,\omega)\ ,

(3.86)

where in the third equality we used the twisted conjugation property of elements in

\mathcal{H}_{\varphi\,\omega\,\varphi^{-1}}
, and in the fourth
equality we used (
3.85
).

Now we define

\displaystyle\Phi:C\ell^{2}(G\rtimes\Gamma)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\mathcal{H}^{\Gamma}\ ,\quad f\longmapsto h^{f}=\sum_{\varphi\in\Gamma}\,h^{f}_{\varphi}\ ,

(3.87)

where
h^{f}_{\varphi}(x)=f(x,\varphi)
.
We show that the image of the map
\Phi
indeed lands in
\mathcal{H}^{\Gamma}
.
First we show that
h^{f}_{\varphi}\in\mathcal{H}_{\varphi}
for
\varphi\in\Gamma
.
Let
x,y\in G
and compute

\displaystyle h^{f}_{\varphi}\big(x\,y\,\varphi(x^{-1})\big):=f\big(x\,y\,\varphi(x^{-1}),\varphi\big)=f\big((x,\text{id}_{G})\,{\textrm{\tiny$\bullet$}}\,(y,\varphi)\,{\textrm{\tiny$\bullet$}}\,(x,\text{id}_{G})^{-1}\big)=f(y,\varphi)=h^{f}_{\varphi}(y)\ ,

(3.88)

where in the third equality we used the twisted conjugation invariance of
f
.
Next we show that
h^{f}
is
\Gamma
-invariant:

\displaystyle(\omega\cdot h^{f})(x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\varphi\in\Gamma}\,(\omega\cdot h^{f}_{\varphi})(x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\varphi\in\Gamma}\,h^{f}_{\varphi}\big(\omega^{-1}(x)\big)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\varphi\in\Gamma}\,f\big(\omega^{-1}(x),\varphi\big)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\varphi\in\Gamma}\,f\big((1,\omega)\,{\textrm{\tiny$\bullet$}}\,(\omega^{-1}(x),\varphi)\,{\textrm{\tiny$\bullet$}}\,(1,\omega)^{-1}\big)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\varphi\in\Gamma}\,f\big(x,\omega\,\varphi\,\omega^{-1}\big)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\varphi^{\prime}\in\Gamma}\,f\big(x,\varphi^{\prime}\big)

\displaystyle=h^{f}(x)\ ,

(3.89)

where in the fourth equality we used again the twisted conjugation
invariance of
f
and changed summation variable in the sixth equality.

Clearly the maps
\Psi
and
\Phi
are inverse to each other.
∎

Theorem 3.90
.

The orbifold theory of two-dimensional Yang-Mills theory with
gauge group
G
and with orbifold defect

\displaystyle\bigoplus_{\varphi\in\Gamma}\,L_{\varphi}

(3.91)

is two-dimensional
Yang-Mills theory with gauge group
G\rtimes\Gamma
.

Proof.

We need to compute the regularised Frobenius algebra structure on
C\ell^{2}(G\rtimes\mathsf{Out}(G))

given by the orbifold theory. This is done by computing the
orbifold theory on the generators of
{\mathscr{B}\mathsf{ord}_{2}^{\hskip 1.0pt\mathrm{area}}}
.
The computations are similar to those for the cylinder in the proof of
Proposition
3.79
, so here we provide less details.

For the cup with area
a
we pick the defect network
and cell decomposition
\omega

\text{id}_{G}
out
A

where we identify the two dashed edges on the two sides. The value of
the state sum area-dependent quantum field theory on this is

\iota_{A}
\displaystyle{\frac{1}{|\Gamma|}\,\sum_{\omega\in\Gamma}}
\mathcal{H}_{\text{id}_{G}}
\pi_{\mathcal{H}_{\text{id}_{G}}}
=\displaystyle{\frac{1}{|\Gamma|}\,\sum_{\omega\in\Gamma}}

\pi_{\mathcal{H}_{\text{id}_{G}}}

(3.92)

We similarly obtain

(3.93)

for the value of the area-dependent quantum field theory on the cap with the defect network and cell decomposition

\iota_{\mathcal{H}_{\varphi}}

\mathcal{H}_{\omega}
\mathcal{H}_{\varphi}

(3.94)

Let
f,g\in C\ell^{2}(G\rtimes\Gamma)\simeq\mathcal{H}^{\Gamma}
with components

f_{\omega}=f(\,\cdot\,,\omega)
and

g_{\omega}=g(\,\cdot\,,\omega)
for
\omega\in\Gamma
. The
morphism in (
3.94
) acts on these functions as

\displaystyle f\otimes g\longmapsto\frac{1}{|\Gamma|^{2}}\,\sum_{\varphi,\omega,\varrho,\nu\in\Gamma}\,\big(f_{\nu\,\omega\,\nu^{-1}}\circ(\varrho\,\varphi\,\varrho^{-1})\big)*g_{\varrho\,\varphi\,\varrho^{-1}}=\sum_{\sigma,\kappa\in\Gamma}\,(f_{\kappa}\circ\sigma)*g_{\sigma}=\sum_{\sigma,\kappa\in\Gamma}\,f_{\sigma^{-1}\,\kappa\,\sigma}*g_{\sigma}

(3.95)

which maps

\displaystyle(x,\gamma)\longmapsto\sum_{\sigma,\kappa\in\Gamma}\,\big(f_{\sigma^{-1}\,\kappa\,\sigma}*g_{\sigma}\big)(x)\,\delta_{\kappa\,\sigma,\gamma}=\sum_{\sigma\in\Gamma}\,\big(f_{\sigma^{-1}\,\gamma}*g_{\sigma}\big)(x)\ ,

(3.96)

as
f_{\sigma^{-1}\,\kappa\,\sigma}*g_{\sigma}\in\mathcal{H}_{\kappa\,\sigma}
.
On the other hand we have

\displaystyle(f*g)(x,\gamma)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{|\Gamma|}\,\sum_{\kappa\in\Gamma}\,\int_{G}\,\mathrm{d}y\ f(y,\kappa)\,g\big((y,\kappa)^{-1}\,{\textrm{\tiny$\bullet$}}\,(x,\gamma)\big)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{|\Gamma|}\,\sum_{\kappa\in\Gamma}\,\int_{G}\,\mathrm{d}y\ f_{\kappa}(y)\,g_{\kappa^{-1}\,\gamma}\big(\kappa^{-1}(y^{-1}\,x)\big)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{|\Gamma|}\,\sum_{\kappa\in\Gamma}\,\int_{G}\,\mathrm{d}y\ f_{\kappa}(y)\,g_{\gamma\,\kappa^{-1}}\big(y^{-1}\,x\big)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{|\Gamma|}\,\sum_{\sigma\in\Gamma}\,\big(f_{\sigma^{-1}\,\gamma}*g_{\sigma}\big)(x)\ .

(3.97)

Altogether we have shown that multiplying by
|\Gamma|
is an
isomorphism of regularised Frobenius algebras

C\ell^{2}(G\rtimes\Gamma)\xrightarrow{\ \simeq\ }\mathcal{H}^{\Gamma}
.
∎

3.5
Orbifold equivalence and the backwards orbifold

We can translate the statement of Theorem
3.90
into an adjoint equivalence in the
orbifold completion
\mathscr{B}_{\textsf{\tiny YM}}^{\mathrm{orb}}

[
CR16
]
of the topological
defect bicategory of two-dimensional Yang-Mills theories

\mathscr{B}_{\textsf{\tiny YM}}
, which is idempotent complete by
Proposition
3.45
. Since the objects of

\mathscr{B}_{\textsf{\tiny YM}}
are bimodules in the symmetric monoidal
category
{\mathscr{H}\mathsf{ilb}}
, and the left and right duality morphisms can be
related by the symmetric braiding, it follows that the defect
bicategory
\mathscr{B}_{\textsf{\tiny YM}}
is pivotal, similarly to the
case of topological field theories.

Proposition 3.98
.

There are adjoint equivalences in
\mathscr{B}_{\textsf{\tiny YM}}^{\mathrm{orb}}
:

\displaystyle{}_{K}K_{L}:\left(L,{}_{L}K_{L}\right)\rightleftarrows\left(K,{}_{K}K_{K}\right):{}_{L}K_{K}\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad{}_{K}K_{L}:\left(L,{}_{L}L_{L}\right)\rightleftarrows\left(K,{}_{K}K\otimes_{L}K_{K}\right):{}_{L}K_{K}\ .

(3.99)

Proof.

Observe that
{}_{L}K_{L}\simeq{}_{L}K\otimes_{K}K_{L}
.
Then use
[
CR16
, Proposition 4.4]
get the second adjoint equivalence.
∎

By
[
CR16
, Proposition 4.3]
,
K\otimes_{L^{2}(G)}K
has
the structure of a strongly separable symmetric Frobenius algebra,
which we will describe in more detail now.
We will see that
K\otimes_{L^{2}(G)}K
is a Wilson line defect,
which is isomorphic to a defect coming from a group symmetry exactly when
\Gamma
is abelian.

(3.107)

The induced action can be computed in a similar way as

K
K
H
L
H
K
\pi\circ\rho^{K\otimes_{L}K}\circ\iota=
H

(3.108)

and after writing out the right action of
L
on
K
we get (
3.101
).

For Part
2

we compute the right
K
-action on
(K\otimes H)_{\mathrm{Wilson}}

for
f,g\in L
and

\delta_{\varphi},\delta_{\omega},\delta_{\gamma}\in H
with
\varphi,\omega,\gamma\in\Gamma
:

\displaystyle(f\otimes\delta_{\varphi}\otimes\delta_{\omega})\cdot(g\otimes\delta_{\gamma})

(3.109)

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt=\left[(x,\sigma)\longmapsto\int_{G}\,\mathrm{d}y\ \frac{1}{|\Gamma|}\,\sum_{\kappa\in\Gamma}\,f\big(x\,(\sigma\,\kappa^{-1})(y^{-1})\big)\,\delta_{\varphi}\big(\sigma\,\kappa^{-1}\big)\,\big(\kappa^{-1}\cdot\delta_{\omega})\,g(y)\,\delta_{\gamma}(\kappa)\right]

(3.110)

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt=\left[(x,\sigma)\longmapsto\int_{G}\,\mathrm{d}y\ \frac{1}{|\Gamma|}\,\sum_{\kappa\in\Gamma}\,f\big(x\,\varphi(y^{-1})\big)\,\delta_{\varphi\,\gamma}(\sigma)\,\big(\gamma^{-1}\cdot\delta_{\omega}\big)\,g(y)\,\delta_{\gamma}(\kappa)\right]

(3.111)

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt=\left[(x,\sigma)\longmapsto\frac{1}{|\Gamma|}\,\int_{G}\,\mathrm{d}y\ f\big(x\,\varphi(y^{-1})\big)\delta_{\varphi\,\gamma}(\sigma)\,\delta_{\omega\,\gamma}\,g(y)\right]

(3.112)

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt=\left[(x,\sigma)\longmapsto\frac{1}{|\Gamma|}\,\int_{G}\,\mathrm{d}z\ f\big(x\,z^{-1}\big)\,\delta_{\varphi\,\gamma}(\sigma)\,\delta_{\omega\,\gamma}\,g\big(\varphi^{-1}(z)\big)\right]

(3.113)

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt=\big(f*(\delta_{\varphi}\cdot g)\big)\otimes\delta_{\varphi\,\gamma}\otimes\delta_{\omega\,\gamma}\ ,

(3.114)

where in the first step we used the delta-functions, in the
second step we used the action of
\Gamma
on
H
, and finally
the invariance of the integral.
This is exactly the right
K
-action in (
3.102
).

For Part
3
, we use
Part
1
to read off the right
K
-action
on
(K\otimes H)_{\mathrm{ind}}
to be

\displaystyle(f\otimes\delta_{\varphi}\otimes\delta_{\omega})\cdot(g\otimes\delta_{\gamma})=\big(f*(\delta_{\varphi\,\omega}\cdot g)\big)\otimes\delta_{\varphi}\otimes\delta_{\omega\,\gamma}\ .

(3.115)

Since
\Psi
obviously commutes with the left
K
-actions, we only need to show that
\Psi
commutes with the right
K
-actions:

\displaystyle\Psi(f\otimes\delta_{\varphi}\otimes\delta_{\omega})\cdot(g\otimes\delta_{\gamma})

\displaystyle=(f\otimes\delta_{\varphi\,\omega}\otimes\delta_{\omega})\cdot(g\otimes\delta_{\gamma})=\big(f*(\delta_{\varphi\,\omega}\cdot g)\big)\otimes\delta_{\varphi\,\omega\,\gamma}\otimes\delta_{\omega\,\gamma}\ ,

(3.116)

\displaystyle\Psi\big((f\otimes\delta_{\varphi}\otimes\delta_{\omega})\cdot(g\otimes\delta_{\gamma})\big)

\displaystyle=\Psi\big((f*(\delta_{\varphi\,\omega}\cdot g))\otimes\delta_{\varphi}\otimes\delta_{\omega\,\gamma}\big)=\big(f*(\delta_{\varphi\,\omega}\cdot g)\big)\otimes\delta_{\varphi\,\omega\,\gamma}\otimes\delta_{\omega\,\gamma}\ .

(3.117)

Clearly
\Psi
is an isomorphism.

Part
4
follows from the facts that

L^{2}(\Gamma)\simeq\bigoplus_{c\in\widehat{\Gamma}}\,V_{c}^{\oplus v_{c}}
as
\Gamma
-modules, where

v_{c}=\dim V_{c}
and
V_{c}
is a representative of the conjugacy
class
c
, and that
all simple
\Gamma
-modules are one-dimensional if and only if
\Gamma
is abelian.
∎

As a consequence of Propositions
3.98
and
3.100
we get the defect for the backwards orbifold:

Theorem 3.118
.

The orbifold theory of two-dimensional Yang-Mills theory with
gauge group
G\rtimes\Gamma
and with orbifold
Wilson line defect

\displaystyle L^{2}(G\rtimes\Gamma)\otimes L^{2}(\Gamma)

(3.119)

is two-dimensional Yang-Mills theory with gauge group
G
.
